At work that turns into estimates and code forms getting mixed up, so that’s what finally pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty often. I feel like I have to keep moving. I’ll go from job sites to bids, then come home and start rewiring test panels in the workshop. I keep jumping into new home-automation ideas before finishing the last one, because sitting still just feels like I’m wasting time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s reckless, but I do sometimes jump into things before I’ve fully thought them through. It’s more like I get an idea and start moving on it, then later realize I probably should’ve slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in lines or when speaking with others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that as a big problem. Lines can be annoying, sure, but I don’t think I’m usually cutting in or anything. When talking, I might get eager to say something, but I’m not sure if it’s more than normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I can’t stay focused on one estimate or code form like I used to. I’ll be working, then a tool noise or a text comes in, or I get some new idea for the workshop, and I’m off on that. Then I come back and the jobs or paperwork start getting mixed up. It’s been hard to manage my time that way.\n\nDoctor: Were you more fidgety or restless as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember being especially fidgety as a kid, at least not in a way anyone made a big deal about. I’d have to think back more or maybe ask my parents.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you had trouble staying focused or sitting still when you were in school?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t remember it being a clear problem in school. I mean, I probably got bored sometimes like anyone, but I don’t recall teachers saying I couldn’t sit still or focus. I’m not sure though—it’s been a while.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Then I lose track of what I was doing, and jobs start getting mixed up. It’s gotten to the point where I figured I needed to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble focusing at home or in other areas of your life, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not only at work, but work is where it’s really showing up. At home I’ll start on something and then a text, a noise from a tool, or some idea about changing something in the workshop pulls me away. Then I come back and I’m not sure where I left off. At work that turns into estimates and code forms getting mixed up, so that’s what finally pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty often. I feel like I have to keep moving. I’ll go from job sites to bids, then come home and start rewiring test panels in the workshop. I keep jumping into new home-automation ideas before finishing the last one, because sitting still just feels like I’m wasting time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s reckless, but I do sometimes jump into things before I’ve fully thought them through. It’s more like I get an idea and start moving on it, then later realize I probably should’ve slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in lines or when speaking with others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that as a big problem. Lines can be annoying, sure, but I don’t think I’m usually cutting in or anything. When talking, I might get eager to say something, but I’m not sure if it’s more than normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I can’t stay focused on one estimate or code form like I used to. I’ll be working, then a tool noise or a text comes in, or I get some new idea for the workshop, and I’m off on that. Then I come back and the jobs or paperwork start getting mixed up. It’s been hard to manage my time that way.\n\nDoctor: Were you more fidgety or restless as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember being especially fidgety as a kid, at least not in a way anyone made a big deal about. I’d have to think back more or maybe ask my parents.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you had trouble staying focused or sitting still when you were in school?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t remember it being a clear problem in school. I mean, I probably got bored sometimes like anyone, but I don’t recall teachers saying I couldn’t sit still or focus. I’m not sure though—it’s been a while.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any difficulties with behavior or learning when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember having major behavior problems or learning issues as a kid. I got through school okay from what I remember, but I don’t have a clear answer beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever mention that you had trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were younger?\n\nPatient: Not that I can remember. I don’t recall my parents or teachers making a point of saying I couldn’t pay attention or sit still. If it came up, it wasn’t something that stuck with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with focus and restlessness is something that has been a part of your life since childhood, or does it seem more like it started when you were older?\n\nPatient: It feels more like it started when I was older. I don’t remember it being a childhood thing. It’s really been building over the last seven months or so, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment.\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit any of these focus or restlessness issues before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: No, not that I know of. I don’t remember having those issues before 12. This feels more recent—like it’s been building over the last seven months, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these issues with focus and restlessness in social situations or when you're relaxing with family and friends, not just at work or in your workshop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not only at work, but that’s where I notice it the most. I can’t stay on one estimate or a code form anymore without getting pulled off by a tool noise, a text, or some new idea about the workshop. Then I lose my place and jobs start getting mixed up. With family or friends, I can get distracted too, but the work stuff is where it’s really causing problems.\n\nDoctor: Did your trouble with focus and restlessness start after a significant life change or stressor, like taking on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s about right. It started after we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment. It’s been building for maybe seven months now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. At work that turns into estimates and code forms getting mixed up, so that’s what finally pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty often. I feel like I have to keep moving. I’ll go from job sites to bids, then come home and start rewiring test panels in the workshop. I keep jumping into new home-automation ideas before finishing the last one, because sitting still just feels like I’m wasting time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s reckless, but I do sometimes jump into things before I’ve fully thought them through. It’s more like I get an idea and start moving on it, then later realize I probably should’ve slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in lines or when speaking with others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that as a big problem. Lines can be annoying, sure, but I don’t think I’m usually cutting in or anything. When talking, I might get eager to say something, but I’m not sure if it’s more than normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I can’t stay focused on one estimate or code form like I used to. I’ll be working, then a tool noise or a text comes in, or I get some new idea for the workshop, and I’m off on that. Then I come back and the jobs or paperwork start getting mixed up. It’s been hard to manage my time that way.\n\nDoctor: Were you more fidgety or restless as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember being especially fidgety as a kid, at least not in a way anyone made a big deal about. I’d have to think back more or maybe ask my parents.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you had trouble staying focused or sitting still when you were in school?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t remember it being a clear problem in school. I mean, I probably got bored sometimes like anyone, but I don’t recall teachers saying I couldn’t sit still or focus. I’m not sure though—it’s been a while.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any difficulties with behavior or learning when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember having major behavior problems or learning issues as a kid. I got through school okay from what I remember, but I don’t have a clear answer beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever mention that you had trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were younger?\n\nPatient: Not that I can remember. I don’t recall my parents or teachers making a point of saying I couldn’t pay attention or sit still. If it came up, it wasn’t something that stuck with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with focus and restlessness is something that has been a part of your life since childhood, or does it seem more like it started when you were older?\n\nPatient: It feels more like it started when I was older. I don’t remember it being a childhood thing. It’s really been building over the last seven months or so, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment.\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit any of these focus or restlessness issues before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: No, not that I know of. I don’t remember having those issues before 12. This feels more recent—like it’s been building over the last seven months, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these issues with focus and restlessness in social situations or when you're relaxing with family and friends, not just at work or in your workshop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not only at work, but that’s where I notice it the most. I can’t stay on one estimate or a code form anymore without getting pulled off by a tool noise, a text, or some new idea about the workshop. Then I lose my place and jobs start getting mixed up. With family or friends, I can get distracted too, but the work stuff is where it’s really causing problems.\n\nDoctor: Did your trouble with focus and restlessness start after a significant life change or stressor, like taking on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s about right. It started after we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment. It’s been building for maybe seven months now.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any symptoms like these before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t remember having anything like this before I was 12. Like I said, it feels like this has really only been building over the last seven months, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m having a hard time staying focused at work. I’ll be trying to do an estimate or fill out a code form, and then a tool noise, or a text, or even some new idea about my workshop pulls me away. Then I lose track of what I was doing, and jobs start getting mixed up. It’s gotten to the point where I figured I needed to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble focusing at home or in other areas of your life, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not only at work, but work is where it’s really showing up. At home I’ll start on something and then a text, a noise from a tool, or some idea about changing something in the workshop pulls me away. Then I come back and I’m not sure where I left off. At work that turns into estimates and code forms getting mixed up, so that’s what finally pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty often. I feel like I have to keep moving. I’ll go from job sites to bids, then come home and start rewiring test panels in the workshop. I keep jumping into new home-automation ideas before finishing the last one, because sitting still just feels like I’m wasting time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s reckless, but I do sometimes jump into things before I’ve fully thought them through. It’s more like I get an idea and start moving on it, then later realize I probably should’ve slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in lines or when speaking with others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that as a big problem. Lines can be annoying, sure, but I don’t think I’m usually cutting in or anything. When talking, I might get eager to say something, but I’m not sure if it’s more than normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I can’t stay focused on one estimate or code form like I used to. I’ll be working, then a tool noise or a text comes in, or I get some new idea for the workshop, and I’m off on that. Then I come back and the jobs or paperwork start getting mixed up. It’s been hard to manage my time that way.\n\nDoctor: Were you more fidgety or restless as a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember being especially fidgety as a kid, at least not in a way anyone made a big deal about. I’d have to think back more or maybe ask my parents.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you had trouble staying focused or sitting still when you were in school?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t remember it being a clear problem in school. I don’t recall my parents or teachers making a point of saying I couldn’t pay attention or sit still. If it came up, it wasn’t something that stuck with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with focus and restlessness is something that has been a part of your life since childhood, or does it seem more like it started when you were older?\n\nPatient: It feels more like it started when I was older. I don’t remember it being a childhood thing. It’s really been building over the last seven months or so, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment.\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit any of these focus or restlessness issues before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: No, not that I know of. I don’t remember having those issues before 12. I can’t stay on one estimate or a code form anymore without getting pulled off by a tool noise, a text, or some new idea about the workshop. Then I lose my place and jobs start getting mixed up. Like I said, it feels like this has really only been building over the last seven months, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe these issues with focus and restlessness as something that definitely started when you were an adult?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it started as an adult. I don’t remember dealing with this as a kid. It’s really been building over the last seven months or so, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment.\n\nDoctor: Did any teachers, family members, or other adults comment on your behavior or ability to focus when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of. I mean, I probably got bored sometimes like anyone, but I don’t recall teachers saying I couldn’t sit still or focus. I’m not sure though—it’s been a while.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any difficulties with behavior or learning when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember having major behavior problems or learning issues as a kid. I got through school okay from what I remember, but I don’t have a clear answer beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever mention that you had trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were younger?\n\nPatient: Not that I can remember. I don’t recall my parents or teachers making a point of saying I couldn’t pay attention or sit still. If it came up, it wasn’t something that stuck with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with focus and restlessness is something that has been a part of your life since childhood, or does it seem more like it started when you were older?\n\nPatient: It feels more like it started when I was older. I don’t remember it being a childhood thing. It’s really been building over the last seven months or so, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment.\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit any of these focus or restlessness issues before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: No, not that I know of. I don’t remember having those issues before 12. This feels more recent—like it’s been building over the last seven months, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these issues with focus and restlessness in social situations or when you're relaxing with family and friends, not just at work or in your workshop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s not only at work, but that’s where I notice it the most. I can’t stay on one estimate or a code form anymore without getting pulled off by a tool noise, a text, or some new idea about the workshop. Then I lose my place and jobs start getting mixed up. With family or friends, I can get distracted too, but the work stuff is where it’s really causing problems.\n\nDoctor: Did your trouble with focus and restlessness start after a significant life change or stressor, like taking on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s about right. It started after we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment. It’s been building for maybe seven months now.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any symptoms like these before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t remember having anything like this before I was 12. Like I said, it feels like this has really only been building over the last seven months, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and equipment.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe these issues with focus and restlessness as something that definitely started when you were an adult?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it started as an adult. I don’t remember dealing with this as a kid.
